Hi, thank you. Well, I came in because I just... I seem to worry about absolutely everything lately. Clients, the housing market, my family, money... even whether moving to the condo was the right decision. Which, you know, downsizing is supposed to make things easier at my age, right? My friend Brenda moved into a condo over in Westlake last year and she just loves it. They have a lovely little pool and a clubhouse where they do water aerobics on Tuesdays. I actually thought about joining a water aerobics class myself, but then I'd have to buy a new swimsuit, and the ones at the department stores are just so poorly made these days. You wash them once and the elastic is completely shot, it's just a waste of money...

No, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding anything, really. I still go to work, show houses, run my errands... though running errands is getting so frustrating lately with the traffic. Last time I sent my nephew to the store for me, he brought back bananas that were practically completely brown...

Well, yes, actually. I do get this terrible tightness. By the end of the day, my neck and shoulders feel so tight, like I've been bracing myself for something that never happens. And I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot, especially when I'm driving between listings or just sitting at my desk. Which reminds me, my desk chair is just awful. I bought it at one of those big office supply stores, you know, the ones that always smell like printer ink and old carpet? The salesman told me it had "ergonomic lumbar support," but honestly, I think it just pokes me right in the spine. I should have kept my old chair, the leather one, but it got a little tear in the armrest when the movers were bringing it into the condo. They were so careless! One of them actually dropped a box of my good china, the set my mother gave me for my wedding. Thank goodness none of the plates broke, just a little chip on one of the teacups, but still... you pay good money for professionals and they just rush through everything. 

But, um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the tension. Yes, my neck and shoulders get very tight, and I definitely clench my jaw. As for sleeping, it's just... well, my mind keeps racing.

Well, yes, I suppose I do. It's like... even when I tell myself, "Parin, this is not urgent," I just can't stop. One concern just leads right into another. Like yesterday, I was worrying about an inspection on a property over on Elm Street, and then I started thinking about how the gutters on my own condo probably need cleaning, which made me think about the HOA fees going up next year. Did I tell you they're raising them by fifteen percent? Fifteen percent! And for what? They say it's for landscaping, but they just hired a new company last month and all they do is blow the leaves around with those terribly loud gas leaf blowers. It's such a racket, especially on Thursday mornings when I'm trying to enjoy my coffee on the patio. I bought this lovely new patio set, wrought iron with these beautiful teal cushions, from a catalog. It took six weeks to arrive, and when it finally did, one of the chairs was missing a little rubber foot, so it wobbled. I had to call customer service three times just to get them to send a replacement part... 

But anyway, before I know it, I've spent half an hour just replaying all these small details in my head that I really meant to just let go of. It's exhausting.

And I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot, especially when I'm driving between listings or just sitting at my desk. Which reminds me, my desk chair is just awful. I bought it at one of those big office supply stores, you know, the ones that always smell like printer ink and old carpet? The salesman told me it had \"ergonomic lumbar support,\" but honestly, I think it just pokes me right in the spine. I should have kept my old chair, the leather one, but it got a little tear in the armrest when the movers were bringing it into the condo. They were so careless! One of them actually dropped a box of my good china, the set my mother gave me for my wedding. Thank goodness none of the plates broke, just a little chip on one of the teacups, but still... you pay good money for professionals and they just rush through everything. \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the tension. Yes, my neck and shoulders get very tight, and I definitely clench my jaw. As for sleeping, it's just... well, my mind keeps racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. It's like... even when I tell myself, \"Parin, this is not urgent,\" I just can't stop. One concern just leads right into another. Like yesterday, I was worrying about an inspection on a property over on Elm Street, and then I started thinking about how the gutters on my own condo probably need cleaning, which made me think about the HOA fees going up next year. Did I tell you they're raising them by fifteen percent? Fifteen percent! And for what? They say it's for landscaping, but they just hired a new company last month and all they do is blow the leaves around with those terribly loud gas leaf blowers. It's such a racket, especially on Thursday mornings when I'm trying to enjoy my coffee on the patio. I bought this lovely new patio set, wrought iron with these beautiful teal cushions, from a catalog. It took six weeks to arrive, and when it finally did, one of the chairs was missing a little rubber foot, so it wobbled. I had to call customer service three times just to get them to send a replacement part... \n\nBut anyway, before I know it, I've spent half an hour just replaying all these small details in my head that I really meant to just let go of. Rituals? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't have to, you know, flip the light switch a certain number of times or wash my hands over and over. I mean, I do like things a certain way when I'm staging a house, of course. You have to make sure the throw pillows are perfectly fluffed. Have you noticed how hard it is to find good throw pillows lately? The ones at the department stores are all so flat now. They use that cheap polyester fill instead of real down. I bought a beautiful set of down-filled cushions for a listing on Maple Drive—oh, it was a gorgeous mid-century modern, sold over asking in two days—but anyway, I left the pillows there by mistake after the open house. The new buyers just kept them! Can you believe that? I called their agent, and he said they thought it was part of the staging that came with the house. People can be so presumptuous. It's like that time I left my favorite umbrella at the hair salon, and when I went back the very next day, the receptionist claimed she hadn't seen it. But it was a very distinctive floral print, you couldn't just miss it...

It's like... even when I tell myself, \"Parin, this is not urgent,\" I just can't stop. One concern just leads right into another. Like yesterday, I was worrying about an inspection on a property over on Elm Street, and then I started thinking about how the gutters on my own condo probably need cleaning, which made me think about the HOA fees going up next year. Did I tell you they're raising them by fifteen percent? Fifteen percent! And for what? They say it's for landscaping, but they just hired a new company last month and all they do is blow the leaves around with those terribly loud gas leaf blowers. It's such a racket, especially on Thursday mornings when I'm trying to enjoy my coffee on the patio. I bought this lovely new patio set, wrought iron with these beautiful teal cushions, from a catalog. It took six weeks to arrive, and when it finally did, one of the chairs was missing a little rubber foot, so it wobbled. I had to call customer service three times just to get them to send a replacement part... \n\nBut anyway, before I know it, I've spent half an hour just replaying all these small details in my head that I really meant to just let go of. It's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Rituals? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't have to, you know, flip the light switch a certain number of times or wash my hands over and over. I mean, I do like things a certain way when I'm staging a house, of course. You have to make sure the throw pillows are perfectly fluffed. Have you noticed how hard it is to find good throw pillows lately? The ones at the department stores are all so flat now. They use that cheap polyester fill instead of real down. I bought a beautiful set of down-filled cushions for a listing on Maple Drive—oh, it was a gorgeous mid-century modern, sold over asking in two days—but anyway, I left the pillows there by mistake after the open house. The new buyers just kept them! Can you believe that? I called their agent, and he said they thought it was part of the staging that came with the house. People can be so presumptuous. It's like that time I left my favorite umbrella at the hair salon, and when I went back the very next day, the receptionist claimed she hadn't seen it. But it was a very distinctive floral print, you couldn't just miss it... \n\nBut, um... no. I don't do any specific behaviors or rituals to stop the worrying. I just kind of sit there and worry.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that you're doing things to avoid triggering your worries?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 61 / real_estate_broker_or_sales_agent / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Avoid things? Well, no, not exactly. I mean, I still go to work, I still do my open houses and meet with clients. I don't hide away or anything. I suppose I try to avoid things that I know are going to be a disaster, like driving on Interstate 95 during rush hour. It's just a parking lot out there, and the other drivers are so aggressive. Last week, this enormous pickup truck—one of those lifted ones with the oversized tires, you know?—he just cut right in front of me without even a turn signal. I had to slam on my brakes so hard my purse flew off the passenger seat, and all my lipsticks spilled out. I had just bought this lovely new shade of coral, too. It rolled right under the seat, and I couldn't reach it for three days until I took the car to the wash. 

And speaking of the car wash, they raised their prices again! It used to be twelve dollars for the basic wash, and now it's eighteen. Eighteen dollars just to run it through those giant blue spinning brushes that I'm half convinced leave tiny scratches on the clear coat anyway. My neighbor, Richard, he washes his own car in his driveway every Saturday morning. He uses this special chamois cloth he ordered from Germany. He's very particular about it. He even offered to wash mine once, but I felt terrible imposing on him, especially since his wife just had knee surgery... 

But, um, avoiding things to stop the worry? No, I don't think I do that. The worry just sort of comes with me wherever I go, even if I'm just sitting at home.

Well, I think we just talked about that, didn't we? The repeating thoughts? I mean, yes, they do repeat. Like I was saying about the gutters and the HOA fees. It's just a constant loop. And speaking of loops, I was trying to knit a scarf the other day—a lovely infinity scarf, you know, the ones that just loop around your neck? I found this beautiful merino wool yarn at a little boutique downtown. It's hand-dyed, a sort of sunset ombre, just gorgeous. But the lady who runs the shop, she's always so chatty. Which sounds lovely, of course, but who can afford a destination wedding these days? The flights alone! I flew to Chicago last month for a real estate conference, and the airlines are just charging for every little thing now. You want a carry-on? That's extra. You want to pick your seat? Extra. I ended up in a middle seat between two men who both wanted the armrests. 

But anyway... yes, the thoughts just keep repeating, even when I try to focus on something else, like my knitting or a client's paperwork. It's very distracting.

Info]\n- demographics: Female / 61 / real_estate_broker_or_sales_agent / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S056\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Rituals? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't have to, you know, flip the light switch a certain number of times or wash my hands over and over. I mean, I do like things a certain way when I'm staging a house, of course. You have to make sure the throw pillows are perfectly fluffed. Have you noticed how hard it is to find good throw pillows lately? The ones at the department stores are all so flat now. They use that cheap polyester fill instead of real down. I bought a beautiful set of down-filled cushions for a listing on Maple Drive—oh, it was a gorgeous mid-century modern, sold over asking in two days—but anyway, I left the pillows there by mistake after the open house. The new buyers just kept them! Can you believe that? I called their agent, and he said they thought it was part of the staging that came with the house. People can be so presumptuous. It's like that time I left my favorite umbrella at the hair salon, and when I went back the very next day, the receptionist claimed she hadn't seen it. But it was a very distinctive floral print, you couldn't just miss it... \n\nBut, um... no. I don't do any specific behaviors or rituals to stop the worrying. I just kind of sit there and worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing things to avoid triggering your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoid things? Well, no, not exactly. I mean, I still go to work, I still do my open houses and meet with clients. I don't hide away or anything. I suppose I try to avoid things that I know are going to be a disaster, like driving on Interstate 95 during rush hour. It's just a parking lot out there, and the other drivers are so aggressive. Last week, this enormous pickup truck—one of those lifted ones with the oversized tires, you know?—he just cut right in front of me without even a turn signal. I had to slam on my brakes so hard my purse flew off the passenger seat, and all my lipsticks spilled out. I had just bought this lovely new shade of coral, too. It rolled right under the seat, and I couldn't reach it for three days until I took the car to the wash. \n\nAnd speaking of the car wash, they raised their prices again! It used to be twelve dollars for the basic wash, and now it's eighteen. Eighteen dollars just to run it through those giant blue spinning brushes that I'm half convinced leave tiny scratches on the clear coat anyway. My neighbor, Richard, he washes his own car in his driveway every Saturday morning. He uses this special chamois cloth he ordered from Germany. He's very particular about it. He even offered to wash mine once, but I felt terrible imposing on him, especially since his wife just had knee surgery... \n\nBut, um, avoiding things to stop the worry? No, I don't think I do that. The worry just sort of comes with me wherever I go, even if I'm just sitting at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Well, I think we just talked about that, didn't we? The repeating thoughts? I mean, yes, they do repeat. Like I was saying about the gutters and the HOA fees. It's just a constant loop. And speaking of loops, I was trying to knit a scarf the other day—a lovely infinity scarf, you know, the ones that just loop around your neck? I found this beautiful merino wool yarn at a little boutique downtown. It's hand-dyed, a sort of sunset ombre, just gorgeous. But the lady who runs the shop, she's always so chatty. I went in just for one skein, and she kept me there for forty-five minutes talking about her daughter's wedding in Tuscany. Which sounds lovely, of course, but who can afford a destination wedding these days? The flights alone! I flew to Chicago last month for a real estate conference, and the airlines are just charging for every little thing now. You want a carry-on? That's extra. You want to pick your seat? Extra. I ended up in a middle seat between two men who both wanted the armrests. \n\nBut anyway... yes, the thoughts just keep repeating, even when I try to focus on something else, like my knitting or a client's paperwork. It's very distracting.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any areas of your life where you don't tend to worry?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Areas where I don't worry? Hmm... I don't think so. I really can't think of any part of my life where I'm not worrying about something. It just seems to touch everything, you know? Like, even when I'm gardening, which is supposed to be relaxing, I'm worrying about the aphids on my roses. I bought this organic neem oil spray because I don't want to use harsh chemicals, especially with the neighbor's cat always wandering into my yard. He's a beautiful orange tabby, named Marmalade. Such a sweet boy, but he loves to dig in my freshly mulched flower beds. I tried putting down cayenne pepper to keep him away—I read that on a gardening blog—but it just washed away in the rain we had on Tuesday. 

Did you see how hard it rained? The storm drains on my street backed up completely. The city really needs to come out and clear the leaves out of the grates. I called public works about it three times last fall, and you just sit on hold listening to that awful elevator music. It’s that same looping jazz track that the dentist’s office plays. I had to get a crown replaced last month because I cracked it on a popcorn kernel at the movies. Twelve dollars for a small popcorn, and it cost me a thousand-dollar dental bill! The movies just aren't worth it anymore. 

But, um, what were you asking? Oh, right, parts of my life without worry. No, I really don't think there are any. It’s just always there.

Last time I sent my nephew to the store for me, he brought back bananas that were practically completely brown... \n\nBut, um, no, to answer your question, I'm not staying home or avoiding places. I do get this terrible tightness. By the end of the day, my neck and shoulders feel so tight, like I've been bracing myself for something that never happens. And I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot, especially when I'm driving between listings or just sitting at my desk. Which reminds me, my desk chair is just awful. I bought it at one of those big office supply stores, you know, the ones that always smell like printer ink and old carpet? The salesman told me it had \"ergonomic lumbar support,\" but honestly, I think it just pokes me right in the spine. One of them actually dropped a box of my good china, the set my mother gave me for my wedding. Oh, right, the tension. Yes, my neck and shoulders get very tight, and I definitely clench my jaw. Like yesterday, I was worrying about an inspection on a property over on Elm Street, and then I started thinking about how the gutters on my own condo probably need cleaning, which made me think about the HOA fees going up next year. Did I tell you they're raising them by fifteen percent? Fifteen percent! And for what? They say it's for landscaping, but they just hired a new company last month and all they do is blow the leaves around with those terribly loud gas leaf blowers. It took six weeks to arrive, and when it finally did, one of the chairs was missing a little rubber foot, so it wobbled. I had to call customer service three times just to get them to send a replacement part... \n\nBut anyway, before I know it, I've spent half an hour just replaying all these small details in my head that I really meant to just let go of. And I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot, especially when I'm driving between listings or just sitting at my desk. Which reminds me, my desk chair is just awful. I bought it at one of those big office supply stores, you know, the ones that always smell like printer ink and old carpet? The salesman told me it had \"ergonomic lumbar support,\" but honestly, I think it just pokes me right in the spine. I should have kept my old chair, the leather one, but it got a little tear in the armrest when the movers were bringing it into the condo. They were so careless! One of them actually dropped a box of my good china, the set my mother gave me for my wedding. Thank goodness none of the plates broke, just a little chip on one of the teacups, but still... you pay good money for professionals and they just rush through everything. \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the tension. Yes, my neck and shoulders get very tight, and I definitely clench my jaw. As for sleeping, it's just... well, my mind keeps racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. It's like... even when I tell myself, \"Parin, this is not urgent,\" I just can't stop. One concern just leads right into another. Like yesterday, I was worrying about an inspection on a property over on Elm Street, and then I started thinking about how the gutters on my own condo probably need cleaning, which made me think about the HOA fees going up next year. Did I tell you they're raising them by fifteen percent? Fifteen percent! And for what? They say it's for landscaping, but they just hired a new company last month and all they do is blow the leaves around with those terribly loud gas leaf blowers. It's such a racket, especially on Thursday mornings when I'm trying to enjoy my coffee on the patio. I bought this lovely new patio set, wrought iron with these beautiful teal cushions, from a catalog. It took six weeks to arrive, and when it finally did, one of the chairs was missing a little rubber foot, so it wobbled. I had to call customer service three times just to get them to send a replacement part... \n\nBut anyway, before I know it, I've spent half an hour just replaying all these small details in my head that I really meant to just let go of. It's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Rituals? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't have to, you know, flip the light switch a certain number of times or wash my hands over and over. I mean, I do like things a certain way when I'm staging a house, of course. You have to make sure the throw pillows are perfectly fluffed. Have you noticed how hard it is to find good throw pillows lately? The ones at the department stores are all so flat now. They use that cheap polyester fill instead of real down. I bought a beautiful set of down-filled cushions for a listing on Maple Drive—oh, it was a gorgeous mid-century modern, sold over asking in two days—but anyway, I left the pillows there by mistake after the open house. The new buyers just kept them! Can you believe that? I called their agent, and he said they thought it was part of the staging that came with the house. People can be so presumptuous. It's like that time I left my favorite umbrella at the hair salon, and when I went back the very next day, the receptionist claimed she hadn't seen it. But it was a very distinctive floral print, you couldn't just miss it... \n\nBut, um... no. I don't do any specific behaviors or rituals to stop the worrying. I just kind of sit there and worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing things to avoid triggering your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoid things? Well, no, not exactly. I mean, I still go to work, I still do my open houses and meet with clients. I don't hide away or anything. I suppose I try to avoid things that I know are going to be a disaster, like driving on Interstate 95 during rush hour. It's just a parking lot out there, and the other drivers are so aggressive. Last week, this enormous pickup truck—one of those lifted ones with the oversized tires, you know?—he just cut right in front of me without even a turn signal. I had to slam on my brakes so hard my purse flew off the passenger seat, and all my lipsticks spilled out. I had just bought this lovely new shade of coral, too. It rolled right under the seat, and I couldn't reach it for three days until I took the car to the wash. \n\nAnd speaking of the car wash, they raised their prices again! It used to be twelve dollars for the basic wash, and now it's eighteen. Eighteen dollars just to run it through those giant blue spinning brushes that I'm half convinced leave tiny scratches on the clear coat anyway. My neighbor, Richard, he washes his own car in his driveway every Saturday morning. He uses this special chamois cloth he ordered from Germany. He's very particular about it. He even offered to wash mine once, but I felt terrible imposing on him, especially since his wife just had knee surgery... \n\nBut, um, avoiding things to stop the worry? No, I don't think I do that. The worry just sort of comes with me wherever I go, even if I'm just sitting at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Well, I think we just talked about that, didn't we? The repeating thoughts? I mean, yes, they do repeat. Like I was saying about the gutters and the HOA fees. It's just a constant loop. And speaking of loops, I was trying to knit a scarf the other day—a lovely infinity scarf, you know, the ones that just loop around your neck? I found this beautiful merino wool yarn at a little boutique downtown. It's hand-dyed, a sort of sunset ombre, just gorgeous. But the lady who runs the shop, she's always so chatty. I went in just for one skein, and she kept me there for forty-five minutes talking about her daughter's wedding in Tuscany. Which sounds lovely, of course, but who can afford a destination wedding these days? The flights alone! I flew to Chicago last month for a real estate conference, and the airlines are just charging for every little thing now. You want a carry-on? That's extra. You want to pick your seat? Extra. I ended up in a middle seat between two men who both wanted the armrests. \n\nBut anyway... yes, the thoughts just keep repeating, even when I try to focus on something else, like my knitting or a client's paperwork. It's very distracting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you don't tend to worry?\n\nPatient: Areas where I don't worry? Hmm... I don't think so. I really can't think of any part of my life where I'm not worrying about something. It just seems to touch everything, you know? Like, even when I'm gardening, which is supposed to be relaxing, I'm worrying about the aphids on my roses. I bought this organic neem oil spray because I don't want to use harsh chemicals, especially with the neighbor's cat always wandering into my yard. He's a beautiful orange tabby, named Marmalade. Such a sweet boy, but he loves to dig in my freshly mulched flower beds. I tried putting down cayenne pepper to keep him away—I read that on a gardening blog—but it just washed away in the rain we had on Tuesday. \n\nDid you see how hard it rained? The storm drains on my street backed up completely. The city really needs to come out and clear the leaves out of the grates. I called public works about it three times last fall, and you just sit on hold listening to that awful elevator music. It’s that same looping jazz track that the dentist’s office plays. I had to get a crown replaced last month because I cracked it on a popcorn kernel at the movies. Twelve dollars for a small popcorn, and it cost me a thousand-dollar dental bill! The movies just aren't worth it anymore. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, right, parts of my life without worry. No, I really don't think there are any. It’s just always there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Intrusive thoughts? You mean like... things that just pop into my head that I don't want there? No, not really. I mean, my worries are always there, like I said, but they're about real things, you know? Not like... weird or crazy thoughts that I have to push away. 

Although, speaking of pushing things away, I had to completely reorganize my pantry this weekend because I found pantry moths. Can you believe it? I think they came in a bag of organic flour I bought at that new health food store on Elm Street. The store is lovely, very clean, but they have all those bulk bins where anyone can just scoop things out. I usually try to buy everything pre-packaged, but I was making a special sourdough starter. My sister-in-law, Brenda, she started making sourdough during the pandemic and never stopped. She brings a loaf to every family gathering. Honestly, it's a bit dense for my taste, and last Thanksgiving it was practically burnt on the bottom, but nobody says anything because she's so proud of it. 

Anyway, I had to throw out all my grains and pastas. It broke my heart to toss three boxes of imported linguine. I had to scrub every shelf with white vinegar. Vinegar is supposed to be a natural deterrent, you know. I use it for cleaning my windows, too, mixed with a little water and a drop of dish soap. It leaves them completely streak-free, unlike those blue commercial sprays that just smear the dirt around. I learned that trick from my grandmother... 

But, no, I don't get those kinds of thoughts. Just the regular worrying about everything.

Did I tell you they're raising them by fifteen percent? Fifteen percent! And for what? They say it's for landscaping, but they just hired a new company last month and all they do is blow the leaves around with those terribly loud gas leaf blowers. It's such a racket, especially on Thursday mornings when I'm trying to enjoy my coffee on the patio. I bought this lovely new patio set, wrought iron with these beautiful teal cushions, from a catalog. It took six weeks to arrive, and when it finally did, one of the chairs was missing a little rubber foot, so it wobbled. I had to call customer service three times just to get them to send a replacement part... \n\nBut anyway, before I know it, I've spent half an hour just replaying all these small details in my head that I really meant to just let go of. It's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Rituals? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't have to, you know, flip the light switch a certain number of times or wash my hands over and over. I mean, I do like things a certain way when I'm staging a house, of course. You have to make sure the throw pillows are perfectly fluffed. Have you noticed how hard it is to find good throw pillows lately? The ones at the department stores are all so flat now. They use that cheap polyester fill instead of real down. I bought a beautiful set of down-filled cushions for a listing on Maple Drive—oh, it was a gorgeous mid-century modern, sold over asking in two days—but anyway, I left the pillows there by mistake after the open house. The new buyers just kept them! Can you believe that? I called their agent, and he said they thought it was part of the staging that came with the house. People can be so presumptuous. It's like that time I left my favorite umbrella at the hair salon, and when I went back the very next day, the receptionist claimed she hadn't seen it. But it was a very distinctive floral print, you couldn't just miss it... \n\nBut, um... no. I don't do any specific behaviors or rituals to stop the worrying. I just kind of sit there and worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing things to avoid triggering your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoid things? Well, no, not exactly. I mean, I still go to work, I still do my open houses and meet with clients. I don't hide away or anything. I suppose I try to avoid things that I know are going to be a disaster, like driving on Interstate 95 during rush hour. It's just a parking lot out there, and the other drivers are so aggressive. Last week, this enormous pickup truck—one of those lifted ones with the oversized tires, you know?—he just cut right in front of me without even a turn signal. I had to slam on my brakes so hard my purse flew off the passenger seat, and all my lipsticks spilled out. I had just bought this lovely new shade of coral, too. It rolled right under the seat, and I couldn't reach it for three days until I took the car to the wash. \n\nAnd speaking of the car wash, they raised their prices again! It used to be twelve dollars for the basic wash, and now it's eighteen. Eighteen dollars just to run it through those giant blue spinning brushes that I'm half convinced leave tiny scratches on the clear coat anyway. My neighbor, Richard, he washes his own car in his driveway every Saturday morning. He uses this special chamois cloth he ordered from Germany. He's very particular about it. He even offered to wash mine once, but I felt terrible imposing on him, especially since his wife just had knee surgery... \n\nBut, um, avoiding things to stop the worry? No, I don't think I do that. The worry just sort of comes with me wherever I go, even if I'm just sitting at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Well, I think we just talked about that, didn't we? The repeating thoughts? I mean, yes, they do repeat. Like I was saying about the gutters and the HOA fees. It's just a constant loop. And speaking of loops, I was trying to knit a scarf the other day—a lovely infinity scarf, you know, the ones that just loop around your neck? I found this beautiful merino wool yarn at a little boutique downtown. It's hand-dyed, a sort of sunset ombre, just gorgeous. But the lady who runs the shop, she's always so chatty. I went in just for one skein, and she kept me there for forty-five minutes talking about her daughter's wedding in Tuscany. Which sounds lovely, of course, but who can afford a destination wedding these days? The flights alone! I flew to Chicago last month for a real estate conference, and the airlines are just charging for every little thing now. You want a carry-on? That's extra. You want to pick your seat? Extra. I ended up in a middle seat between two men who both wanted the armrests. \n\nBut anyway... yes, the thoughts just keep repeating, even when I try to focus on something else, like my knitting or a client's paperwork. It's very distracting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you don't tend to worry?\n\nPatient: Areas where I don't worry? Hmm... I don't think so. I really can't think of any part of my life where I'm not worrying about something. It just seems to touch everything, you know? Like, even when I'm gardening, which is supposed to be relaxing, I'm worrying about the aphids on my roses. I bought this organic neem oil spray because I don't want to use harsh chemicals, especially with the neighbor's cat always wandering into my yard. He's a beautiful orange tabby, named Marmalade. Such a sweet boy, but he loves to dig in my freshly mulched flower beds. I tried putting down cayenne pepper to keep him away—I read that on a gardening blog—but it just washed away in the rain we had on Tuesday. \n\nDid you see how hard it rained? The storm drains on my street backed up completely. The city really needs to come out and clear the leaves out of the grates. I called public works about it three times last fall, and you just sit on hold listening to that awful elevator music. It’s that same looping jazz track that the dentist’s office plays. I had to get a crown replaced last month because I cracked it on a popcorn kernel at the movies. Twelve dollars for a small popcorn, and it cost me a thousand-dollar dental bill! The movies just aren't worth it anymore. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, right, parts of my life without worry. No, I really don't think there are any. It’s just always there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Intrusive thoughts? You mean like... things that just pop into my head that I don't want there? No, not really. I mean, my worries are always there, like I said, but they're about real things, you know? Not like... weird or crazy thoughts that I have to push away. \n\nAlthough, speaking of pushing things away, I had to completely reorganize my pantry this weekend because I found pantry moths. Can you believe it? I think they came in a bag of organic flour I bought at that new health food store on Elm Street. The store is lovely, very clean, but they have all those bulk bins where anyone can just scoop things out. I usually try to buy everything pre-packaged, but I was making a special sourdough starter. My sister-in-law, Brenda, she started making sourdough during the pandemic and never stopped. She brings a loaf to every family gathering. Honestly, it's a bit dense for my taste, and last Thanksgiving it was practically burnt on the bottom, but nobody says anything because she's so proud of it. \n\nAnyway, I had to throw out all my grains and pastas. It broke my heart to toss three boxes of imported linguine. I had to scrub every shelf with white vinegar. Vinegar is supposed to be a natural deterrent, you know. I use it for cleaning my windows, too, mixed with a little water and a drop of dish soap. It leaves them completely streak-free, unlike those blue commercial sprays that just smear the dirt around. I learned that trick from my grandmother... \n\nBut, no, I don't get those kinds of thoughts. Just the regular worrying about everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Well, I think we just talked about that, didn't we? The repeating thoughts? I mean, yes, they do repeat. Like I was saying about the gutters and the HOA fees. It's just a constant loop. I found this beautiful merino wool yarn at a little boutique downtown. It's hand-dyed, a sort of sunset ombre, just gorgeous. But the lady who runs the shop, she's always so chatty. I really can't think of any part of my life where I'm not worrying about something. It just seems to touch everything, you know? Like, even when I'm gardening, which is supposed to be relaxing, I'm worrying about the aphids on my roses. I bought this organic neem oil spray because I don't want to use harsh chemicals, especially with the neighbor's cat always wandering into my yard. He's a beautiful orange tabby, named Marmalade. Such a sweet boy, but he loves to dig in my freshly mulched flower beds. I tried putting down cayenne pepper to keep him away—I read that on a gardening blog—but it just washed away in the rain we had on Tuesday. \n\nDid you see how hard it rained? The storm drains on my street backed up completely. The city really needs to come out and clear the leaves out of the grates. I called public works about it three times last fall, and you just sit on hold listening to that awful elevator music. It’s that same looping jazz track that the dentist’s office plays. I had to get a crown replaced last month because I cracked it on a popcorn kernel at the movies. Twelve dollars for a small popcorn, and it cost me a thousand-dollar dental bill! The movies just aren't worth it anymore. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, right, parts of my life without worry. No, I really don't think there are any. It’s just always there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Intrusive thoughts? You mean like... things that just pop into my head that I don't want there? No, not really. I mean, my worries are always there, like I said, but they're about real things, you know? Not like... weird or crazy thoughts that I have to push away. \n\nAlthough, speaking of pushing things away, I had to completely reorganize my pantry this weekend because I found pantry moths. Can you believe it? I think they came in a bag of organic flour I bought at that new health food store on Elm Street. The store is lovely, very clean, but they have all those bulk bins where anyone can just scoop things out. I usually try to buy everything pre-packaged, but I was making a special sourdough starter. My sister-in-law, Brenda, she started making sourdough during the pandemic and never stopped. She brings a loaf to every family gathering. Honestly, it's a bit dense for my taste, and last Thanksgiving it was practically burnt on the bottom, but nobody says anything because she's so proud of it. \n\nAnyway, I had to throw out all my grains and pastas. It broke my heart to toss three boxes of imported linguine. I had to scrub every shelf with white vinegar. Vinegar is supposed to be a natural deterrent, you know. I use it for cleaning my windows, too, mixed with a little water and a drop of dish soap. It leaves them completely streak-free, unlike those blue commercial sprays that just smear the dirt around. I learned that trick from my grandmother... \n\nBut, no, I don't get those kinds of thoughts. Just the regular worrying about everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your worrying is interfering with your ability to work or enjoy time with friends and family?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 61 / real_estate_broker_or_sales_agent / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work, even straightforward listing negotiations and contract reviews are taking me much longer than they used to, and I’ve had to delay returning calls because I don’t feel confident I’ve checked everything carefully enough.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose it is. Especially at work. Even straightforward listing negotiations and contract reviews are taking me much longer than they used to. I had this one listing last week, a beautiful mid-century modern split-level over on Oak Creek Drive. The sellers are retiring to Florida. Sarasota, I think. Sarasota has that lovely Ringling Museum with the miniature circus. I took my niece there when she was ten. She's twenty-four now and working in digital marketing, whatever that means. She tries to explain it to me, but it just sounds like she looks at her phone all day. 

But anyway, the contract for that house was so simple, just standard boilerplate, but I kept reading it over and over. I’ve actually had to delay returning client calls because I just don’t feel confident I’ve checked everything carefully enough. And speaking of calls, the amount of spam calls I get on my cell phone lately is just outrageous. They always tell me my car warranty is expiring. I drive a 2021 Lexus, I know exactly when my warranty expires! I had to download one of those call-blocking apps, but it blocked my sister by mistake. She was furious because she was trying to reach me to organize our cousin's baby shower. We're doing a Peter Rabbit theme. So classic, don't you think? Much better than those gender reveal parties with the colored smoke that always seem to start forest fires. People just don't think about the environmental impact of those things. 

But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, work. Yes, it’s definitely interfering because I just can't seem to get through my paperwork without second-guessing myself.

The new buyers just kept them! Can you believe that? I called their agent, and he said they thought it was part of the staging that came with the house. People can be so presumptuous. It's like that time I left my favorite umbrella at the hair salon, and when I went back the very next day, the receptionist claimed she hadn't seen it. But it was a very distinctive floral print, you couldn't just miss it... \n\nBut, um... no. I don't do any specific behaviors or rituals to stop the worrying. It used to be twelve dollars for the basic wash, and now it's eighteen. My neighbor, Richard, he washes his own car in his driveway every Saturday morning. He uses this special chamois cloth he ordered from Germany. He's very particular about it. He even offered to wash mine once, but I felt terrible imposing on him, especially since his wife just had knee surgery... \n\nBut, um, avoiding things to stop the worry? No, I don't think I do that. The worry just sort of comes with me wherever I go, even if I'm just sitting at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Well, I think we just talked about that, didn't we? The repeating thoughts? I mean, yes, they do repeat. Like I was saying about the gutters and the HOA fees. It's just a constant loop. And speaking of loops, I was trying to knit a scarf the other day—a lovely infinity scarf, you know, the ones that just loop around your neck? I found this beautiful merino wool yarn at a little boutique downtown. It's hand-dyed, a sort of sunset ombre, just gorgeous. But the lady who runs the shop, she's always so chatty. I went in just for one skein, and she kept me there for forty-five minutes talking about her daughter's wedding in Tuscany. Which sounds lovely, of course, but who can afford a destination wedding these days? The flights alone! I flew to Chicago last month for a real estate conference, and the airlines are just charging for every little thing now. You want a carry-on? That's extra. You want to pick your seat? Extra. I ended up in a middle seat between two men who both wanted the armrests. \n\nBut anyway... yes, the thoughts just keep repeating, even when I try to focus on something else, like my knitting or a client's paperwork. It's very distracting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you don't tend to worry?\n\nPatient: Areas where I don't worry? Hmm... I don't think so. I really can't think of any part of my life where I'm not worrying about something. It just seems to touch everything, you know? Like, even when I'm gardening, which is supposed to be relaxing, I'm worrying about the aphids on my roses. I bought this organic neem oil spray because I don't want to use harsh chemicals, especially with the neighbor's cat always wandering into my yard. He's a beautiful orange tabby, named Marmalade. Such a sweet boy, but he loves to dig in my freshly mulched flower beds. I tried putting down cayenne pepper to keep him away—I read that on a gardening blog—but it just washed away in the rain we had on Tuesday. \n\nDid you see how hard it rained? The storm drains on my street backed up completely. The city really needs to come out and clear the leaves out of the grates. I called public works about it three times last fall, and you just sit on hold listening to that awful elevator music. It’s that same looping jazz track that the dentist’s office plays. I had to get a crown replaced last month because I cracked it on a popcorn kernel at the movies. Twelve dollars for a small popcorn, and it cost me a thousand-dollar dental bill! The movies just aren't worth it anymore. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, right, parts of my life without worry. No, I really don't think there are any. It’s just always there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Intrusive thoughts? You mean like... things that just pop into my head that I don't want there? No, not really. I mean, my worries are always there, like I said, but they're about real things, you know? Not like... weird or crazy thoughts that I have to push away. \n\nAlthough, speaking of pushing things away, I had to completely reorganize my pantry this weekend because I found pantry moths. Can you believe it? I think they came in a bag of organic flour I bought at that new health food store on Elm Street. The store is lovely, very clean, but they have all those bulk bins where anyone can just scoop things out. I usually try to buy everything pre-packaged, but I was making a special sourdough starter. My sister-in-law, Brenda, she started making sourdough during the pandemic and never stopped. She brings a loaf to every family gathering. Honestly, it's a bit dense for my taste, and last Thanksgiving it was practically burnt on the bottom, but nobody says anything because she's so proud of it. \n\nAnyway, I had to throw out all my grains and pastas. It broke my heart to toss three boxes of imported linguine. I had to scrub every shelf with white vinegar. Vinegar is supposed to be a natural deterrent, you know. I use it for cleaning my windows, too, mixed with a little water and a drop of dish soap. It leaves them completely streak-free, unlike those blue commercial sprays that just smear the dirt around. I learned that trick from my grandmother... \n\nBut, no, I don't get those kinds of thoughts. Just the regular worrying about everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your ability to work or enjoy time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is. Especially at work. Even straightforward listing negotiations and contract reviews are taking me much longer than they used to. I had this one listing last week, a beautiful mid-century modern split-level over on Oak Creek Drive. The sellers are retiring to Florida. Sarasota, I think. Sarasota has that lovely Ringling Museum with the miniature circus. I took my niece there when she was ten. She's twenty-four now and working in digital marketing, whatever that means. Worry-free? No, not at all. Honestly, that's exactly why I came in to see you. I just seem to worry about absolutely everything now—my clients, the housing market, my family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision. And I know it feels completely out of proportion to what is actually happening, but I just can't stop it. 

Like with the condo... I thought downsizing would mean less stress, but the homeowner's association is just terribly strict. They have rules about everything. You can't even have a welcome mat that isn't pre-approved by the board! I had bought this lovely coir mat with little bluebirds on it from a craft fair up in Vermont. My friend Susan and I go every October for the foliage. The leaves were a bit dull this year, though. Someone said it was because we didn't get enough rain in August. We stayed at this quaint little bed and breakfast, but the mattress was like sleeping on a slab of granite. My back ached for a week after we got home. I tried taking Epsom salt baths—the Dr. Teal's lavender one, which usually helps so much. I always buy it in bulk at Costco. Have you seen how crowded Costco gets on a Saturday? You can barely get your cart down the aisle, especially around the sample stations. People just abandon their carts right in the middle of the walkway for a tiny piece of a frozen taquito... 

But, um, what were you asking me? Oh, right, an activity where I don't worry. No. No, there really isn't one. It just follows me everywhere.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Well, I came in because I just... I seem to worry about absolutely everything lately. Clients, the housing market, my family, money... even whether moving to the condo was the right decision. Which, you know, downsizing is supposed to make things easier at my age, right? My friend Brenda moved into a condo over in Westlake last year and she just loves it. They have a lovely little pool and a clubhouse where they do water aerobics on Tuesdays. I actually thought about joining a water aerobics class myself, but then I'd have to buy a new swimsuit, and the ones at the department stores are just so poorly made these days. You wash them once and the elastic is completely shot, it's just a waste of money... \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, the worrying. It just... it all feels so out of proportion to what is actually happening in my life right now. I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or places because of your worries?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding anything, really. I still go to work, show houses, run my errands... though running errands is getting so frustrating lately with the traffic. They started that construction on 4th Street, and it is just a nightmare. I swear, they've been working on that same stretch of road since last summer. My sister, she lives out in the suburbs, and she keeps telling me I should just order my groceries online to save time. Have you ever tried that? The delivery services? She says it's wonderful, but I just don't trust someone else to pick out my produce. I mean, how do they know if an avocado is perfectly ripe? You have to squeeze it just right. Last time I sent my nephew to the store for me, he brought back bananas that were practically completely brown... \n\nBut, um, no, to answer your question, I'm not staying home or avoiding places. I'm just worrying the whole time I'm out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your body when you're worrying, like feeling tense or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I do get this terrible tightness. By the end of the day, my neck and shoulders feel so tight, like I've been bracing myself for something that never happens. And I catch myself clenching my jaw a lot, especially when I'm driving between listings or just sitting at my desk. Which reminds me, my desk chair is just awful. I bought it at one of those big office supply stores, you know, the ones that always smell like printer ink and old carpet? The salesman told me it had \"ergonomic lumbar support,\" but honestly, I think it just pokes me right in the spine. I should have kept my old chair, the leather one, but it got a little tear in the armrest when the movers were bringing it into the condo. They were so careless! One of them actually dropped a box of my good china, the set my mother gave me for my wedding. Thank goodness none of the plates broke, just a little chip on one of the teacups, but still... you pay good money for professionals and they just rush through everything. \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the tension. Yes, my neck and shoulders get very tight, and I definitely clench my jaw. As for sleeping, it's just... well, my mind keeps racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. It's like... even when I tell myself, \"Parin, this is not urgent,\" I just can't stop. One concern just leads right into another. Like yesterday, I was worrying about an inspection on a property over on Elm Street, and then I started thinking about how the gutters on my own condo probably need cleaning, which made me think about the HOA fees going up next year. Did I tell you they're raising them by fifteen percent? Fifteen percent! And for what? They say it's for landscaping, but they just hired a new company last month and all they do is blow the leaves around with those terribly loud gas leaf blowers. It's such a racket, especially on Thursday mornings when I'm trying to enjoy my coffee on the patio. I bought this lovely new patio set, wrought iron with these beautiful teal cushions, from a catalog. It took six weeks to arrive, and when it finally did, one of the chairs was missing a little rubber foot, so it wobbled. I had to call customer service three times just to get them to send a replacement part... \n\nBut anyway, before I know it, I've spent half an hour just replaying all these small details in my head that I really meant to just let go of. It's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Rituals? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't have to, you know, flip the light switch a certain number of times or wash my hands over and over. I mean, I do like things a certain way when I'm staging a house, of course. You have to make sure the throw pillows are perfectly fluffed. Have you noticed how hard it is to find good throw pillows lately? The ones at the department stores are all so flat now. They use that cheap polyester fill instead of real down. I bought a beautiful set of down-filled cushions for a listing on Maple Drive—oh, it was a gorgeous mid-century modern, sold over asking in two days—but anyway, I left the pillows there by mistake after the open house. The new buyers just kept them! Can you believe that? I called their agent, and he said they thought it was part of the staging that came with the house. People can be so presumptuous. It's like that time I left my favorite umbrella at the hair salon, and when I went back the very next day, the receptionist claimed she hadn't seen it. But it was a very distinctive floral print, you couldn't just miss it... \n\nBut, um... no. I don't do any specific behaviors or rituals to stop the worrying. I just kind of sit there and worry.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing things to avoid triggering your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoid things? Well, no, not exactly. I mean, I still go to work, I still do my open houses and meet with clients. I don't hide away or anything. I suppose I try to avoid things that I know are going to be a disaster, like driving on Interstate 95 during rush hour. It's just a parking lot out there, and the other drivers are so aggressive. Last week, this enormous pickup truck—one of those lifted ones with the oversized tires, you know?—he just cut right in front of me without even a turn signal. I had to slam on my brakes so hard my purse flew off the passenger seat, and all my lipsticks spilled out. I had just bought this lovely new shade of coral, too. It rolled right under the seat, and I couldn't reach it for three days until I took the car to the wash. \n\nAnd speaking of the car wash, they raised their prices again! It used to be twelve dollars for the basic wash, and now it's eighteen. Eighteen dollars just to run it through those giant blue spinning brushes that I'm half convinced leave tiny scratches on the clear coat anyway. My neighbor, Richard, he washes his own car in his driveway every Saturday morning. He uses this special chamois cloth he ordered from Germany. He's very particular about it. He even offered to wash mine once, but I felt terrible imposing on him, especially since his wife just had knee surgery... \n\nBut, um, avoiding things to stop the worry? No, I don't think I do that. The worry just sort of comes with me wherever I go, even if I'm just sitting at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Well, I think we just talked about that, didn't we? The repeating thoughts? I mean, yes, they do repeat. Like I was saying about the gutters and the HOA fees. It's just a constant loop. And speaking of loops, I was trying to knit a scarf the other day—a lovely infinity scarf, you know, the ones that just loop around your neck? I found this beautiful merino wool yarn at a little boutique downtown. It's hand-dyed, a sort of sunset ombre, just gorgeous. But the lady who runs the shop, she's always so chatty. I went in just for one skein, and she kept me there for forty-five minutes talking about her daughter's wedding in Tuscany. Which sounds lovely, of course, but who can afford a destination wedding these days? The flights alone! I flew to Chicago last month for a real estate conference, and the airlines are just charging for every little thing now. You want a carry-on? That's extra. You want to pick your seat? Extra. I ended up in a middle seat between two men who both wanted the armrests. \n\nBut anyway... yes, the thoughts just keep repeating, even when I try to focus on something else, like my knitting or a client's paperwork. It's very distracting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you don't tend to worry?\n\nPatient: Areas where I don't worry? Hmm... I don't think so. I really can't think of any part of my life where I'm not worrying about something. It just seems to touch everything, you know? Like, even when I'm gardening, which is supposed to be relaxing, I'm worrying about the aphids on my roses. I bought this organic neem oil spray because I don't want to use harsh chemicals, especially with the neighbor's cat always wandering into my yard. He's a beautiful orange tabby, named Marmalade. Such a sweet boy, but he loves to dig in my freshly mulched flower beds. I tried putting down cayenne pepper to keep him away—I read that on a gardening blog—but it just washed away in the rain we had on Tuesday. \n\nDid you see how hard it rained? The storm drains on my street backed up completely. The city really needs to come out and clear the leaves out of the grates. I called public works about it three times last fall, and you just sit on hold listening to that awful elevator music. It’s that same looping jazz track that the dentist’s office plays. I had to get a crown replaced last month because I cracked it on a popcorn kernel at the movies. Twelve dollars for a small popcorn, and it cost me a thousand-dollar dental bill! The movies just aren't worth it anymore. \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, right, parts of my life without worry. No, I really don't think there are any. It’s just always there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Intrusive thoughts? You mean like... things that just pop into my head that I don't want there? No, not really. I mean, my worries are always there, like I said, but they're about real things, you know? Not like... weird or crazy thoughts that I have to push away. \n\nAlthough, speaking of pushing things away, I had to completely reorganize my pantry this weekend because I found pantry moths. Can you believe it? I think they came in a bag of organic flour I bought at that new health food store on Elm Street. The store is lovely, very clean, but they have all those bulk bins where anyone can just scoop things out. I usually try to buy everything pre-packaged, but I was making a special sourdough starter. My sister-in-law, Brenda, she started making sourdough during the pandemic and never stopped. She brings a loaf to every family gathering. Honestly, it's a bit dense for my taste, and last Thanksgiving it was practically burnt on the bottom, but nobody says anything because she's so proud of it. \n\nAnyway, I had to throw out all my grains and pastas. It broke my heart to toss three boxes of imported linguine. I had to scrub every shelf with white vinegar. Vinegar is supposed to be a natural deterrent, you know. I use it for cleaning my windows, too, mixed with a little water and a drop of dish soap. It leaves them completely streak-free, unlike those blue commercial sprays that just smear the dirt around. I learned that trick from my grandmother... \n\nBut, no, I don't get those kinds of thoughts. Just the regular worrying about everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your ability to work or enjoy time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is. Especially at work. Even straightforward listing negotiations and contract reviews are taking me much longer than they used to. I had this one listing last week, a beautiful mid-century modern split-level over on Oak Creek Drive. The sellers are retiring to Florida. Sarasota, I think. Sarasota has that lovely Ringling Museum with the miniature circus. I took my niece there when she was ten. She's twenty-four now and working in digital marketing, whatever that means. She tries to explain it to me, but it just sounds like she looks at her phone all day. \n\nBut anyway, the contract for that house was so simple, just standard boilerplate, but I kept reading it over and over. I’ve actually had to delay returning client calls because I just don’t feel confident I’ve checked everything carefully enough. And speaking of calls, the amount of spam calls I get on my cell phone lately is just outrageous. They always tell me my car warranty is expiring. I drive a 2021 Lexus, I know exactly when my warranty expires! I had to download one of those call-blocking apps, but it blocked my sister by mistake. She was furious because she was trying to reach me to organize our cousin's baby shower. We're doing a Peter Rabbit theme. So classic, don't you think? Much better than those gender reveal parties with the colored smoke that always seem to start forest fires. People just don't think about the environmental impact of those things. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, work. Yes, it’s definitely interfering because I just can't seem to get through my paperwork without second-guessing myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there even a single activity or part of your daily routine where you find yourself completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Worry-free? No, not at all. Honestly, that's exactly why I came in to see you. I just seem to worry about absolutely everything now—my clients, the housing market, my family, money, even whether moving to the condo was the right decision. And I know it feels completely out of proportion to what is actually happening, but I just can't stop it. \n\nLike with the condo... I thought downsizing would mean less stress, but the homeowner's association is just terribly strict. They have rules about everything. You can't even have a welcome mat that isn't pre-approved by the board! I had bought this lovely coir mat with little bluebirds on it from a craft fair up in Vermont. My friend Susan and I go every October for the foliage. The leaves were a bit dull this year, though. Someone said it was because we didn't get enough rain in August. We stayed at this quaint little bed and breakfast, but the mattress was like sleeping on a slab of granite. My back ached for a week after we got home. I tried taking Epsom salt baths—the Dr. Teal's lavender one, which usually helps so much. I always buy it in bulk at Costco. Have you seen how crowded Costco gets on a Saturday? You can barely get your cart down the aisle, especially around the sample stations. People just abandon their carts right in the middle of the walkway for a tiny piece of a frozen taquito... \n\nBut, um, what were you asking me? Oh, right, an activity where I don't worry. No. No, there really isn't one. It just follows me everywhere.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Restless? I suppose so, yes. I just feel like I can't ever truly settle down. It's like this constant buzzing feeling under my skin, you know? Just always wound up. 

It actually reminds me of my old rescue dog, Buster. He was a terrier mix, the sweetest thing, but he had this terrible anxiety during thunderstorms. He would pace the hallways for hours, just back and forth, clicking his little nails on the hardwood floors. Click, click, click. We tried everything for him. Those little thunder shirts? Completely useless. I ended up having to buy him these special calming treats with chamomile in them. I got them from this little boutique pet store downtown. The owner there, Margaret, she used to be a real estate agent too, actually. Small world, isn't it? She left the business back in 2008 when the market crashed. Said she couldn't handle the stress of short sales anymore. I can't blame her, really. Short sales are a nightmare of paperwork. You have to deal with the banks, and they move at a glacial pace. I had one last year that took nine months to close! Nine months! The buyers almost walked away three times. They ended up having a baby before they even got the keys to the house. A little boy, I think. Or maybe it was a girl? They sent me a birth announcement, but the picture was so blurry it was hard to tell. People really rely too much on their phone cameras these days. My niece tried to tell me the new iPhone takes better pictures than a professional camera, but I just don't believe it. You need a proper lens for good portraits. 

But anyway, yes, restless. That's exactly how I feel. Just like poor Buster pacing the floors.

It's like that time I left my favorite umbrella at the hair salon, and when I went back the very next day, the receptionist claimed she hadn't seen it.
